</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p><strong>Background/Objectives:</strong> <em>Eupatorium lindleyanum</em> DC (Eup), a traditional Chinese medicinal herb, is widely used for treating inflammation-mediated diseases, including pneumonia. However, its potential therapeutic effects on inflammation-driven liver fibrosis remain to be elucidated. This study aimed to investigate the effects of Eup on carbon tetrachloride (CCl<sub>4</sub>)-induced liver fibrosis and elucidate its underlying mechanisms. <strong>Methods:</strong> The chemical constituents of Eup were analyzed using ultra-performance liquid chromatography coupled with quadrupole time-of-flight mass spectrometry (UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS). A CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis murine model and LX-2 cells were used in study. Serum biochemical assays, histological analysis, qRT-PCR, ELISA, and Western blot were used to assess Eup’s anti-inflammatory and anti-fibrotic properties. RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) was employed to identify potential mechanisms, with validation by Western blot. <strong>Results:</strong> 89 and 49 compounds were identified in Eup under positive and negative ion modes, respectively. <em>In vivo</em>, Eup treatment decreased collagen deposition and expression levels of fibrosis-related genes, including collagen I and α-smooth muscle actin. Additionally, Eup alleviated hepatic inflammation. <em>In vitro</em>, Eup inhibited FBS-induced hepatic stellate cell (HSCs) activation. Gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) indicated that Eup significantly downregulated the platelet-derived growth factor (PDGF)/platelet-derived growth factor receptor-beta (PDGFR-β) signaling pathway, which was further validated in both CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced fibrotic livers and PDGF-BB-activated HSCs using western blot. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Eup attenuated liver fibrosis by inhibiting inflammation and suppressing HSCs activation via downregulating PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> liver fibrosis, <em>Eupatorium lindleyanum</em> DC, inflammation, PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling, hepatic stellate cell</p></section></section><section id="sec1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Hepatic fibrosis, a common pathological outcome of chronic liver disease (CLD), is marked by excessive extracellular matrix (ECM) deposition and abnormal scar tissue formation [<a href="#B1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">1</a>]. It is primarily triggered by persistent liver injury caused by factors such as chronic hepatitis B or C infection, alcohol abuse, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD), and cholestasis [<a href="#B2-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">2</a>,<a href="#B3-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">3</a>]. Without effective intervention, liver fibrosis can progress to irreversible cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), significantly impairing patient prognosis and quality of life. Globally, liver-related diseases account for approximately 2 million deaths annually, representing 4% of total mortality, with cirrhosis and HCC being the leading causes [<a href="#B4-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">4</a>]. Despite its significant health burden, the molecular mechanisms underlying liver fibrosis remain incompletely understood, and there is a pressing need for safe and effective therapeutic agents to halt or reverse its progression.</p>
<p>Liver fibrosis, resulting from CLD, represents a reparative response characterized by the excessive and disorganized accumulation of collagen produced by activated HSCs and their myofibroblast derivatives [<a href="#B5-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">5</a>]. In the context of repeated injury, damaged hepatocytes, infiltrating immune cells, resident macrophages, and HSCs secrete various paracrine and autocrine growth factors and inflammatory chemokines, perpetuating a cycle of tissue damage and remodeling that results in the formation of a fibrotic matrix. The injury to hepatocytes initiates an inflammatory reaction, activating macrophages, releasing reactive oxygen species (ROS), transforming growth factor-beta 1 (TGFβ1) [<a href="#B6-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">6</a>], and converting dormant HSCs into myofibroblast-like cells [<a href="#B7-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">7</a>]. Once activated, HSCs/myofibroblasts proliferate in response to cytokines such as PDGF, express fibrogenic markers like α-SMA, secrete type I collagen, and contribute to liver fibrosis [<a href="#B8-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">8</a>]. When the underlying cause of injury is resolved, myofibroblasts either undergo apoptosis or revert to an inactive state. Therefore, inhibiting the activation of HSCs or eliminating fibrogenic myofibroblasts are key therapeutic approaches to prevent the progression of fibrosis [<a href="#B9-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">9</a>]. Targeting liver lipid metabolism, oxidative stress, inflammation, and cell death may represent an alternative therapeutic approach for hepatic fibrosis [<a href="#B10-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">10</a>].</p>
<p>Several key pathways contribute to HSC activation, including TGF-β signaling pathway, PDGF signaling pathway, Hippo signaling pathway, and ROS [<a href="#B11-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">11</a>]. Among these, the PDGF signaling pathway exhibits specificity for HSCs. The PDGF family comprises four ligands (PDGF A–D) that signal through dimeric transmembrane receptors, PDGFR-α, and PDGFR-β [<a href="#B12-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">12</a>]. While PDGFR-α expression remains stable in both quiescent and activated HSCs, PDGFR-β expression correlates with the severity of liver fibrosis [<a href="#B13-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">13</a>,<a href="#B14-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">14</a>]. Ligand binding induces receptor dimerization and tyrosine phosphorylation, activating downstream pathways such as p38 MAPK, PI3K/AKT, and Ras/tyrosine kinase, which promote HSCs’ proliferation and survival [<a href="#B15-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">15</a>,<a href="#B16-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">16</a>,<a href="#B17-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">17</a>]. Notably, genetic depletion of PDGFR-β in HSCs has been shown to attenuate liver injury and fibrosis <em>in vivo</em> [<a href="#B18-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">18</a>]. Enhanced PDGF-BB secretion by L02 cells has been shown to promote LX-2 cell activation in co-culture systems [<a href="#B19-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">19</a>]. Furthermore, compounds such as Salvianolic acid B [<a href="#B20-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">20</a>], Roseotoxin B [<a href="#B21-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">21</a>], and Gomisin D [<a href="#B22-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">22</a>] have demonstrated anti-fibrotic effects by targeting the PDGF/PDGFR-β pathway, underscoring its therapeutic potential.</p>
<p><em>Eupatorium lindleyanum</em> DC (Eup) (plant name was checked at <a href="http://www.theplantlist.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.theplantlist.org</a>), commonly known as “Yemazhui”, is a traditional Chinese medicine herb classified under the genus Eupatorium in the Compositae family, widely distributed in China, with Jiangsu Province being its authentic producing area [<a href="#B23-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">23</a>,<a href="#B24-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">24</a>]. Historically, Eup has been used by local communities to treat respiratory conditions such as cough and tracheitis [<a href="#B25-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">25</a>]. More than 100 bioactive ingredients have been identified in Eup, including triterpenoids, sesquiterpenes, diterpenoids, organic acids, flavonoids, volatile oils, and amino acids [<a href="#B26-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">26</a>]. Substantial differences in chemical composition were observed across distinct medicinal parts [<a href="#B27-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">27</a>]. Eup is traditionally recognized for its properties of relieving cough, dispelling phlegm, clearing heat, and detoxifying [<a href="#B28-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">28</a>]. It has garnered attention for its anti-inflammatory [<a href="#B25-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">25</a>,<a href="#B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">29</a>], anti-tumor [<a href="#B30-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">30</a>,<a href="#B31-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">31</a>], and antioxidant effects [<a href="#B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">29</a>,<a href="#B32-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">32</a>]. However, its potential role in hepatic fibrosis remains unexplored.</p>
<p>In this study, we aimed to investigate the anti-fibrotic efforts of Eup and elucidate its underlying mechanism. To investigate this, we utilized a CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced mouse model of liver fibrosis and the LX-2 human hepatic stellate cell line to assess the pharmacological impact of Eup. RNA sequencing was employed to identify the pathways modulated by Eup. Our findings aim to offer a scientific foundation for advancing Eup as a novel therapeutic agent for liver fibrosis, bridging traditional medicinal use with modern pharmacological evidence.</p></section><section id="sec2-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Chemical Profiling of Eup Extract</h3>
<p>The chemical constituents of the Eup extract were analyzed using UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS. In total, 89 distinct compounds were characterized in positive ion mode, while 49 compounds were identified under negative ion detection conditions (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>A,B). The detailed components were listed in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>. Among these components, Citric acid can improve LPS-induced liver injury [<a href="#B33-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">33</a>], and Embelin demonstrated protective effects against thioacetamide-induced acute hepatic injury in murine models [<a href="#B34-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">34</a>]. Moreover, a recent study has shown that oxalic acid can protect cells from oxidative stress [<a href="#B35-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">35</a>]. Based on the presence of these hepatoprotective components, Eup may have an ameliorative effect on liver fibrosis.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/08da6e585401/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="933" width="726" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Chemical profiling of Eup extract and transcriptomic analysis of RNA sequencing data. (<strong>A</strong>) Representative UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS total ion chromatogram of Eup obtained in negative ionization mode. (<strong>B</strong>) Corresponding UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS total ion chromatogram of Eup acquired in positive ionization mode. (<strong>C</strong>) PCA of RNA-seq data from the NC group and the 40 g/kg Eup group (n = 3 per group). (<strong>D</strong>) Volcano plot visualization of DEGs comparing the NC group and the 40 g/kg Eup-treated group. Significantly upregulated genes are denoted by red data points, while downregulated genes are indicated by blue data points. (n = 3 per group; DEGs with adjusted <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05). (<strong>E</strong>) Heatmap and hierarchical clustering showing the separation of the NC group and the 40 g/kg Eup group, n = 3 per group.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec2dot2-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Bioinformatics Analysis of mRNA-Seq Gene</h3>
<p>RNA sequencing analysis was performed on liver tissues from the normal control group (n = 3) and the 40 g/kg Eup group (n = 3) using the Illumina NovaSeq 6000 platform (Illumina, San Diego, CA, USA). PCA of gene expression profiles revealed distinct clustering patterns between the control group and the 40 g/kg Eup group (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>C). Similarly, samples from the control group and the 40 g/kg Eup group were separately clustered in a Clustered Heatmap (<a href="#app1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link">Figure S3</a>). Differential expression analysis identified 1294 significantly regulated genes (|log2FC| &gt; 1, FDR &lt; 0.05), comprising 704 upregulated and 590 downregulated genes in the 40 g/kg Eup group compared to controls (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>D). Gene Ontology (GO) enrichment analysis of these differentially expressed genes demonstrated significant enrichment in biological processes, including collagen biosynthetic process, regulation of collagen biosynthetic process, myoblast differentiation, and acute inflammatory response (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>E). These enrichment terms are highly associated with fibrogenesis, suggesting Eup may possess potential therapeutic efficacy in ameliorating hepatic fibrosis.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Eup Attenuates CCl<sub>4</sub>-Induced Liver Fibrosis in Mice</h3>
<p>To assess the anti-fibrotic effects of Eup, seven-week-old mice were intraperitoneally injected with CCl<sub>4</sub> (2:5 <em>v</em>/<em>v</em> in corn oil) or the same volume of corn oil every two days. Meanwhile, each group was administered Eup (4, 16, and 40 g/kg) or vehicle daily (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A). Body weight measurements (initial and final), food consumption data, and hepatic weight parameters are presented in <a href="#app1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figure S1A–E</a>. The liver index in the model group was significantly higher compared to the control group. Macroscopic examination revealed distinct morphological differences between groups (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B): control livers exhibited normal reddish coloration and smooth surfaces, whereas CCl<sub>4</sub>-treated livers displayed dark discoloration and coarse granular surfaces. Notably, 16 g/kg and 40 g/kg Eup treatment groups showed marked improvement in liver color and surface texture. Histopathological analysis by H&amp;E staining demonstrated characteristic features of liver fibrosis in the CCl<sub>4</sub> model group, including extensive hepatocyte necrosis, inflammatory cell infiltration, disruption of hepatic cord architecture, and portal/perisinusoidal fibrosis (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>C). Quantitative fibrotic scoring revealed significant attenuation of these pathological changes in Eup-treated groups (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>D). Consistent with histological findings, the level of serum ALT and AST in Eup treatment groups exhibited a decreased trend (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>E), while the level of serum ALP was significantly decreased after Eup treatment (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>F).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/5f95e9dfff72/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="726" width="712" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The potential of Eup to improve the murine liver fibrosis induced by CCl<sub>4</sub>. (<strong>A</strong>) Flow chart of CCl<sub>4</sub>-treated mice treated with gavage Eup or drinking water. Seven-week-old mice were intraperitoneally injected with CCl<sub>4</sub> (2:5 <em>v</em>/<em>v</em> in corn oil) or the same volume of corn oil every two days. Each group was gavaged with Eup (4, 16, and 40 g/kg) or vehicle daily. After 5 weeks of treatment, the livers were collected from these mice. (<strong>B</strong>) Morphological observation of mouse livers. (<strong>C</strong>,<strong>D</strong>) H&amp;E staining was performed to assess histological alterations in liver tissue sections across experimental groups. Representative views and the quantification results are presented; scale bar, 50 μm. (<strong>E</strong>,<strong>F</strong>) Serum concentrations of ALT, AST, and ALP were quantified. The statistical significances of different grades were defined as <sup>###</sup>
<em>p</em> &lt; 0.001 versus control group; ** <em>p</em> &lt; 0.01 and *** <em>p</em> &lt; 0.001 versus CCl<sub>4</sub> group. The “ns” indicates non-significant (<em>p</em> ≥ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure><p>Consequently, we aim to conduct further investigations into the therapeutic potential of Eup in treating CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced hepatic fibrosis. Sirius-red staining indicated a notable reduction of collagen deposition, with the improvement of the formation of pseudolobules after the administration of Eup. (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>A,B). The qRT-PCR analysis revealed significantly elevated mRNA expression levels of <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, <em>Col4</em>, <em>LOX</em>, and <em>TGF-β</em> in the model group. Notably, administration of medium and high doses of the treatment resulted in marked downregulation of these fibrotic markers, with <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, and <em>Col4</em> demonstrating particularly significant reductions (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>C–G). Protein expression analysis through Western blot corroborated these observations at the translational level, showing that Eup (40 g/kg) treatment significantly suppressed CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced upregulation of α-SMA and collagen I (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>H–J). These proteins are well-established markers of HSC activation and fibrogenesis [<a href="#B36-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">36</a>]. The transcriptomic analysis further supported these observations, with hierarchical clustering revealing the downregulation of fibrogenic genes (<em>Col1a1</em>, <em>Timp1</em>, <em>Tgfbr1</em>) and upregulation of matrix degradation genes (<em>Mmp2</em>, <em>Mmp9</em>) in the 40 g/kg Eup group compared to the control group (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>K). These findings indicate that Eup has anti-fibrotic effects in the murine model.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/ad0916c64700/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="974" width="779" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>

The “ns” indicates non-significant (<em>p</em> ≥ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot4-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Eup Treatment Therapeutically Mitigates the Inflammation Progression of CCl<sub>4</sub>-Evoked Hepatic Fibrosis</h3>
<p>To figure out the function of Eup in hepatic inflammation, the immunohistochemical staining method was applied. There was an increase in the number of CD86 + macrophages or MPO + neutrophils in CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced fibrotic livers in comparison with the untreated group, which was remarkably converted by Eup administration in a dose-dependent manner (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>A–C). Consistent patterns were detected from pro-inflammatory mediators, specifically TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6, through ELISA quantification (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>E). Moreover, the number of CD163 + macrophages was increased in the fibrotic liver after Eup treatment (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>A,D). The mRNA expression levels of <em>IL-10</em>, an anti-inflammatory factor, were significantly upregulated after Eup treatment (4 g/kg) (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>H). Additionally, Transcriptomic heatmap analysis confirmed systemic downregulation of inflammatory mediators in the Eup group (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>F). Consistently, GSEA further revealed significant inhibition of key inflammatory pathways. Similarly, the regulation of lymphocyte activation and positive regulation of cytokine production were downgraded as well after administering Eup (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>G and <a href="#app1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link">Figure S1G–I</a>). All of these experimental data imply that Eup can significantly attenuate the hepatic inflammatory response in CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced fibrotic livers in mice.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/c5bf19269b7f/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="908" width="725" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>

The “ns” indicates non-significant (<em>p</em> ≥ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot5-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Eup Treatment Inhibits HSC Activation In Vitro</h3>
<p>To evaluate the impact of Eup on HSC activation, an <em>in vitro</em> experimental model was established through 24-h incubation of the LX-2 cells with 10% FBS (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>A). CCK-8 assay revealed that the maximum safe concentration of Eup was 20 μg/mL in LX-2 cells (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>B). To assess the therapeutic effect of Eup at different concentrations on HSCs activation, intracellular mRNA expression was quantitatively examined by qRT-PCR analysis. As shown in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>C–F, the mRNA levels of fibrosis-related indicators, including <em>α-SMA</em>, <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, and <em>LOX</em>, in LX-2 cells were significantly increased after incubating with 10% FBS, indicating that 10% FBS can significantly induce HSC activation. Nevertheless, treating with Eup decreased the mRNA levels of Col1, Col3, and LOX significantly. Thereinto, the mRNA expression of Col1 declined in a dose-dependent manner. While qRT-PCR analysis showed a modest decrease in α-SMA mRNA expression, Western blot quantification demonstrated significant downregulation of α-SMA protein expression with Eup administration (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>G,H). Taken together, these results provide compelling evidence that Eup exerts potent inhibitory effects on HSC activation under <em>in vitro</em> conditions.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/fd8496ae96dd/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1041" width="749" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Eup treatment suppresses HSC activation <em>in vitro</em>. (<strong>A</strong>) Overview of the <em>in vitro</em> experiment. The “ns” indicates non-significant (<em>p</em> ≥ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot6-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Eup Improves CCl<sub>4</sub>-Mediated Murine Liver Fibrosis by Modulating the PDGF/PDGFR-β Signaling Pathway</h3>
<p>RNA-sequencing analysis revealed that treatment with Eup (40 g/kg) significantly downregulated the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway in hepatic tissues (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>A). To further validate these findings, we assessed the phosphorylation levels of PDGFR-β in liver tissues via Western blotting. Our findings revealed that the expressions of p-PDGFR-β/PDGFR-β proteins were significantly higher than those in the normal control group after CCl<sub>4</sub> treatment, whereas administration of 40 g/kg Eup significantly downregulated the ratio of p-PDGFR-β/PDGFR-β proteins in the liver fibrosis model (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>B,C). These results preliminarily indicate that Eup may ameliorate CCl<sub>4</sub>-evoked liver fibrosis by modulating the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway. To further elucidate the downstream mechanisms, we evaluated the PI3K/AKT and MAPK pathways (ERK). CCl<sub>4</sub> exposure significantly increased the p-AKT/AKT and p-ERK/ERK protein ratios compared to the control group. However, Eup administration (40 g/kg) reversed these effects, normalizing both AKT and ERK phosphorylation levels (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>B,D,E). These results suggested that Eup suppressed the downstream signaling pathways of PDGFR-β. Therefore, Eup can ameliorate murine liver fibrosis triggered by CCl<sub>4</sub> through modulating the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/68c045b7988c/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="944" width="730" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>

(<strong>A</strong>) GSEA analyses of PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway between the NC group and the 40 g/kg Eup group (n = 3 per group). GAPDH was the loading control. Data are presented as mean ± SD, n = 3. <sup>#</sup>

Eup Suppresses PDGF-BB-Induced HSCs Activation by Inhibiting the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β Signaling Pathway</h3>
<p><em>In vitro</em>, we utilized PDGF-BB to activate LX-2 cells to further validate the anti-fibrotic mechanism of Eup (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>A). Following the administration of PDGF-BB (20 ng/mL), qRT-PCR results indicated that the mRNA expression levels of <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, <em>α-SMA</em>, and <em>LOX</em> were increased significantly. However, co-treatment with Eup (1, 10, and 20 μg/mL) for 24 h during the PDGF-BB-induced LX-2 cells activation, we observed that Eup (20 μg/mL) treatment significantly reduced the mRNA expression levels of <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, <em>α-SMA</em>, and <em>LOX</em> in LX-2 cells (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>B–E). Consistent with transcriptional changes, Western blotting results showed that PDGF-BB treatment significantly elevated the protein expression level of α-SMA in LX-2 cells, whereas Eup (20 μg/mL) treatment significantly reversed this effect (<a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>F,G). Therefore, Eup can inhibit the activation of HSCs induced by PDGF-BB.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12389491_pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa6c/12389491/a4d1b003b6c3/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="884" width="755" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Eup suppresses PDGF-BB-induced HSC activation by inhibiting the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling pathway. (<strong>A</strong>) LX-2 cells were cultured with 20 ng/mL PDGF-BB, 1, 10, and 20 μg/mL Eup for 24 h. (<strong>B</strong>–<strong>E</strong>) qRT-PCR analysis of <em>α-SMA</em>, <em>Col1</em>, <em>Col3</em>, and <em>LOX</em> in LX-2 cells administrated with different concentrations of Eup. (<strong>F</strong>) Representative immunoblot images demonstrating protein expression of GAPDH, α-SMA, p-PDGFR-β, PDGFR-β, p-AKT, AKT, p-ERK, and ERK. (<strong>G</strong>–<strong>J</strong>) Expression levels of α-SMA and phosphorylation ratios of PDGFR-β (p-PDGFR-β/PDGFR-β), AKT (p-AKT/AKT), and ERK (p-ERK/ERK). The loading control was GAPDH. Data are presented as mean ± SD, n = 3. <sup>#</sup>
<em>p</em> &lt; 0.05, <sup>##</sup>
<em>p</em> &lt; 0.01, and <sup>###</sup>
<em>p</em> &lt; 0.001 versus PDGF-BB (-) Eup (-) group; * <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05 and ** <em>p</em> &lt; 0.01 versus PDGF-BB (+) Eup (-) group.</p></figcaption></figure><p>To further elucidate the mechanism by which Eup suppresses HSC activation, we investigated its potential regulatory effects on the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling cascade through Western blot analysis of key pathway components. As demonstrated in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>F,H–J, stimulation of LX-2 cells with 20 ng/mL PDGF-BB resulted in significant elevation of phosphorylation ratios for PDGFR-β, AKT, and ERK. In contrast, Eup treatment (20 μg/mL) significantly attenuated these phosphorylation events. These results demonstrate that Eup effectively suppresses PDGF-BB-induced HSC activation through inhibition of the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling cascade. Collectively, the data indicate that Eup exerts its anti-fibrotic effects by targeting this pathway to inhibit HSC activation.</p></section></section><section id="sec3-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Discussion</h2>
<p>In the United States, an estimated 80.19 million individuals are afflicted with Steatotic Liver Disease, with 14.32 million exhibiting Clinically Significant Fibrosis (CSF) [<a href="#B37-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">37</a>]. In China, hepatic steatosis and associated fibrosis constitute a substantial health burden [<a href="#B38-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">38</a>]. Unfortunately, there remains a paucity of clinically effective pharmacotherapies for liver fibrosis. Consequently, the development of efficacious interventions is imperative. Current therapeutic strategies for liver fibrosis primarily focus on: (1) inhibition of HSC activation, (2) induction of apoptosis in activated HSCs, (3) reduction of oxidative stress, (4) immune modulation, and (5) inhibition of ECM deposition and scar formation [<a href="#B39-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">39</a>]. Given the multifactorial etiology of liver fibrosis, combination therapies targeting multiple pathways simultaneously are emerging as a key direction for future research [<a href="#B10-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">10</a>]. In this context, traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) offers significant promise due to its multi-target, multi-pathway mechanisms of action, low toxicity, and favorable therapeutic outcomes [<a href="#B40-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">40</a>,<a href="#B41-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">41</a>]. Some Chinese traditional medicines have been shown to have the potential to improve liver fibrosis, such as Paeoniflorin [<a href="#B42-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">42</a>], Notoginsenoside R1 [<a href="#B43-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">43</a>], and Berberine [<a href="#B44-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">44</a>]. Eup, identified in the 1970s, is predominantly utilized for the treatment of respiratory disorders. Empirical evidence suggests that Eup mitigates lung fibrosis and acute lung injury in murine models, and exerts inhibitory effects on the metastatic potential of hepatic and breast carcinomas [<a href="#B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">29</a>,<a href="#B45-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">45</a>,<a href="#B46-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">46</a>]. This study aims to demonstrate that Eup may alleviate liver fibrosis caused by CCl<sub>4</sub> administration in a murine model.</p>
<p>Through UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS, over one hundred components were identified in Eup. Among them, betaine has been shown to ameliorate dimethylnitrosamine (DMN)-induced oxidative liver injury by modulating sulfur-containing metabolite pathways, thereby attenuating oxidative stress and suppressing the progression of liver fibrosis [<a href="#B47-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">47</a>]. Cyanidin is the aglycone moiety of Cyanidin-3-O-β-glucoside. The study demonstrates that Cyanidin-3-O-β-glucoside mitigates oxidative stress, reduces hepatocyte apoptosis, and suppresses hepatic inflammation, ultimately inhibiting HSC activation and preventing CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis [<a href="#B48-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">48</a>]. Furthermore, delphinidin has been reported to reverse progressive hepatic fibrosis by inactivating HSCs. This is achieved through the suppression of pro-fibrotic cytokines like TNF-α and TGF-β, alongside the upregulation of metallothionein I/II (MT I/II) to enhance hepatic regenerative capacity [<a href="#B49-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">49</a>]. Our study systematically confirmed the anti-fibrotic properties of Eup using both <em>in vivo</em> and <em>in vitro</em> experimental approaches. <em>In vivo</em>, Eup administration significantly improved liver histopathology, as evidenced by reduced collagen fiber formation and ECM deposition. <em>In vitro</em>, Eup dramatically prevented the activation of LX-2 cells. Additionally, we noted that treatment with Eup alone did not affect normal liver function in mice, preliminarily indicating the safety of Eup.</p>
<p>Chronic liver inflammation is a primary driver in triggering and advancing the development of liver fibrosis [<a href="#B50-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">50</a>]. Studies have shown that numerous pathways associated with inflammation are activated during the process of liver fibrosis, which promotes the activation of HSCs and the deposition of collagen in the liver [<a href="#B51-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">51</a>]. Given the important role of inflammation in fibrosis, we verified whether Eup could exert an anti-inflammatory effect in CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis. The former study has shown that IL-1β and TNF-α promote the survival of HSCs by mediating the activation of nuclear factor kappa B (NF-κB) within HSCs [<a href="#B52-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">52</a>]. The level of plasma IL-6 may increase with the progression of cirrhosis [<a href="#B53-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">53</a>]. Both resident hepatic macrophages and monocyte-derived macrophages are significant drivers of fibrosis progression. Our results indicated that Eup not only reduced the protein levels of pro-inflammatory cytokines in fibrotic livers, but also decreased the infiltration of neutrophils and monocytes. Macrophages, which play a central role in liver inflammation, can be polarized into two distinct phenotypes: M1 and M2. M1-phenotype macrophages, characterized by high expression of CD80/CD86 and inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS), exert pro-inflammatory effects, whereas M2-phenotype macrophages, marked by high expression of CD163/CD206, exhibit anti-inflammatory and tissue-repair functions [<a href="#B54-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">54</a>]. Previous studies have shown that suppressing M1-phenotype macrophage polarization and stimulating M2-phenotype macrophage polarization alleviated mice liver fibrosis induced by CCl<sub>4</sub> [<a href="#B55-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">55</a>,<a href="#B56-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">56</a>]. In alignment with these findings, our research revealed that Eup treatment increased the number of M2-phenotype macrophages while decreasing the number of M1-phenotype macrophages in the fibrotic livers of mice induced by CCl<sub>4</sub>. Therefore, the data suggest that Eup alleviates hepatic inflammatory responses by improving inflammatory cell infiltration, reducing the production of pro-inflammatory cytokines, and increasing the number of M2-phenotype macrophages, which contributes to the amelioration of liver fibrosis.</p>
<p>Using GSEA, we observed that treatment with Eup alone downregulated the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway in normal mouse liver. Over the past few years, PDGFR has emerged as a widely recognized target for anti-fibrotic therapeutics [<a href="#B20-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">20</a>,<a href="#B21-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">21</a>,<a href="#B22-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">22</a>]. The level of PDGFR-β in liver tissue shows a positive correlation with the severity of liver fibrosis, and it is primarily expressed in activated HSCs [<a href="#B14-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">14</a>]. Activation of PDGFR-β exacerbates liver fibrosis, as evidenced by increased collagen deposition and elevated mRNA expression of α-SMA and collagen I in mice with constitutive PDGFR-β activation [<a href="#B18-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">18</a>]. PDGF-BB, a member of the PDGF family, is a ligand for PDGFR-β and also the most potent mitogen for HSCs. The expression of PDGF-BB is low in normal liver tissue, and the secretion of PDGF-BB is increased from hepatocytes and thrombocytes [<a href="#B57-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">57</a>]. Here, we found that HSCs were remarkably activated after administering PDGF-BB. However, treatment with Eup significantly suppressed HSC activation induced by PDGF-BB. When PDGF-BB binds to the PDGFR-β on HSCs, tyrosine residues of PDGFR-β are phosphorylated, activating downstream signaling pathways including MAPK, PI3K/AKT, and Ras protein/tyrosine-protein kinase pathways [<a href="#B15-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">15</a>,<a href="#B17-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">17</a>,<a href="#B58-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">58</a>]. Activation of the ERK and PI3K/AKT pathways supports the proliferation of HSCs and their differentiation into hepatic fibroblasts [<a href="#B59-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">59</a>]. Similarly, stimulation of the p38 MAP kinase pathway enhances the migration of hepatic myofibroblasts [<a href="#B58-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">58</a>]. In the current study, to further verify whether Eup improved CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced murine liver fibrosis by regulating the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway, we measured the phosphorylation levels of PDGFR-β, AKT, and ERK proteins. The findings indicated that Eup markedly decreased the phosphorylation levels of PDGFR-β, AKT, and ERK proteins both <em>in vivo</em> and <em>in vitro</em>. Collectively, we have confirmed Eup suppressed PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway and its downstream key signaling proteins. Therefore, we conclude that Eup inhibits the activation of HSCs at least in part by regulating the PDGF/PDGFR-β signaling pathway, thereby improving CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis in mice.</p>
<p>However, several limitations should be noted. First, Eup did not significantly ameliorate CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver injury, indicating that its primary therapeutic effects may be specific to fibrotic processes rather than acute hepatocyte damage. These results are consistent with existing studies [<a href="#B60-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">60</a>,<a href="#B61-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">61</a>]. This dissociation suggests that Eup’s primary action may be the selective modulation of HSCs activation, rather than direct protection of hepatocytes. Second, while betaine, cyanidin, and delphinidin have been identified as anti-fibrotic components, the contributions of other bioactive constituents in Eup remain to be elucidated. These bioactive components may exert their anti-fibrotic effects through multi-target mechanisms. Third, the mechanism by which Eup alleviates inflammation requires further investigation. Fourth, Eup treatment downregulated the protein expression of α-SMA, but failed to reduce the transcriptional level. The reason might be that Eup only functions during the translation process of α-SMA. Future studies should focus on isolating and characterizing additional active compounds in Eup, as well as exploring its molecular targets in greater depth to fully understand its therapeutic potential.</p>
<p>In summary, our study demonstrates that Eup significantly ameliorates CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis through dual mechanisms. As displayed in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, firstly, Eup inhibits the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling pathway, which subsequently suppresses the activation of HSCs and improves liver fibrosis. Secondly, Eup attenuates the inflammatory response in fibrotic livers, thereby mitigating the progression of liver fibrosis. The findings may provide potential therapeutic drugs for the clinical treatment of liver fibrosis and offer theoretical evidence for the development of clinical drugs for the treatment of liver fibrosis.</p>

<figcaption><p>The mechanism diagram of Eup in improving CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis. Eup suppressed the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling pathway, thereby inhibiting HSCs activation, and then improving liver fibrosis induced by CCl<sub>4</sub>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec4-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec4dot1-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Preparation of Eup</h3>
<p>Eup was purchased from Guangsheng Trading Co., Ltd. (Baoding, China), as a dried mixture of stems and leaves. Before decoction, the stems of Eup were rod-shaped, approximately 2 cm in length and 0.3 cm in diameter, while the leaf area was about 2 cm<sup>2</sup>. Eup was first soaked in 15 volumes of distilled water for 30 min, and a decoction was made twice for 1.5 h each time at 100 °C. Combine 2 times of water decoction and filter through sterile gauze, then concentrate to Eup containing 5 g crude drug per 1 mL under reduced pressure. The concentrated extract was aliquoted, sealed, and stored at −80 °C. For <em>in vitro</em> experiments, the extract was additionally filtered through a 0.22 µm sterile microporous membrane. The quantitative analysis of the primary constituents in Eup was conducted employing UPLC-Q/TOF-LC/MS. Liquid chromatography (LC) was performed using an Agilent 1290 UPLC system, and mass spectrometry (MS) was conducted using an Agilent Q-TOF 6550 instrument. The chromatographic method involved the use of water containing 0.1% formic acid as mobile phase A and acetonitrile as mobile phase B. The gradient elution program is presented in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>. The electrospray ionization parameters were optimized as follows: 4000 V for positive ion mode and 3200 V for negative ion mode. The sample injection volume was set at 5 µL.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec4dot2-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Animals and Experimental Design</h3>
<p>C57BL male mice (7 weeks; 18–22 g) were sourced from Zolgene Biotechnology Co., Ltd. (Nanjing, China). The housing environment for all mice was carefully controlled, maintaining a temperature of (24 ± 2) °C, relative humidity of (50 ± 5)%, and a pathogen-free condition, with the provision of sufficient food and water ad libitum.</p>
<p>Mice were deeply anesthetized via intraperitoneal injection of tribromoethanol, ensuring complete loss of consciousness and absence of pain perception. Then, euthanasia was immediately performed by cervical dislocation. Immediately post-mortem, dissection was performed. The target liver tissue was rapidly and aseptically excised. The excised liver tissue was promptly immersed in pre-chilled sterile saline or PBS buffer for a brief rinse to remove surface blood. Subsequently, the liver tissue was sectioned into small fragments (&lt;0.5 cm<sup>3</sup>) to facilitate rapid freezing and subsequent homogenization.</p>
<p>The dissected liver fragments were immediately subjected to snap-freezing in liquid nitrogen. Following snap-freezing, the tissue samples were transferred to and stored long-term at −80℃ in an ultra-low temperature freezer until subsequent experimental analysis. All experimental procedures were conducted according to the guidelines approved by the Laboratory Animal Research Center of Fujian Medical University (ethical code: IACUC FJMU 2023-Y-1118, 23 November 2023).</p>
<p>A cohort of 30 mice was utilized in this study. Using GraphPad Prism software (version 9.0), animals underwent complete randomization and were allocated equally into six experimental groups (n = 5 each group): (1) control group, (2) 40 g/kg Eup group, (3) CCl<sub>4</sub> group, (4) CCl<sub>4</sub> + 4 g/kg Eup group, (5) CCl<sub>4</sub> + 16 g/kg Eup group, (6) CCl<sub>4</sub> + 40 g/kg Eup group), each group containing 5 mice. To induce liver fibrosis, CCl<sub>4</sub> (2:5 <em>v</em>/<em>v</em> in corn oil) or an equal volume of corn oil was injected intraperitoneally (i.p.) at a dose of 2 mL/kg body weight (BW) once every two days during the experimental period (5 weeks). Concurrently, each group of mice was administered intragastrically (i.g.) with Eup (4, 16, and 40 g/kg) or an equal volume of a vehicle every day throughout the experimental period. Before sacrifice, the mice underwent a 12-h fasting period. Liver tissues and blood were collected for subsequent analyses. The experimental design is illustrated schematically in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A.</p></section><section id="sec4dot3-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3. Histological Analysis: H&amp;E Staining, Sirius-Red Staining, and Fibrotic Score</h3>
<p>Liver tissues were immersed in 10% phosphate-buffered formalin for fixation, subsequently embedded in paraffin, and sectioned into 4 µm slices for histological examination.</p>
<p>To evaluate collagen deposition, liver tissue sections were stained with Sirius Red (G1472, Beijing Solarbio Science &amp; Technology Co., Ltd., Beijing, China), using a saturated aqueous solution of picric acid combined with Direct Red 80. This staining technique specifically highlights the collagen fibers within the liver tissue, enabling a quantitative analysis of the fibrosis present. The quantitative analysis of the Sirius-red positive areas was executed via ImageJ software (Version 1.54f, National Institutes of Health, Bethesda, MD, USA).</p>
<p>Fibrotic changes were assessed through H&amp;E staining, which was performed on paraffin-embedded tissue sections following standard protocols. The histological scoring system established by Ishak et al. was used to evaluate the stage of liver fibrosis [<a href="#B62-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">62</a>].</p></section><section id="sec4dot4-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.4. Immunohistochemistry (IHC) Staining</h3>
<p>As described before [<a href="#B63-pharmaceuticals-18-01228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-pharmaceuticals-18-01228">63</a>], liver tissues were fixed in 10% phosphate-buffered formalin, subsequently embedded in paraffin, and subjected to standard procedures. Using a microtome, tissue sections with a thickness of 4 µm were cut and subsequently mounted onto glass slides. Before staining, paraffin sections were deparaffinized by immersion in fresh xylene twice for 15 min each, rehydrated (successively immersed in 100%-90%-70%-50% ethanol for 2 min), and immersed in antigen repair solution (citrate) at 100 °C for 30 min. Then, blocking the Non-specific binding was carried out with 3% peroxidase solution at room temperature for 10 min. Afterward, the sections were incubated at 4 °C overnight with the respective primary antibodies. The primary antibodies used for immunohistochemistry included anti-CD86 (1:200), anti-CD163 (1:500), and anti-MPO (1:300). The sections were then incubated with horseradish peroxidase (HRP)-conjugated secondary antibodies at 37 °C for 30 min. Following each incubation step, the sections were rinsed three times with phosphate-buffered saline (PBS). The immune complexes were observed by using the DAB Substrate Kit according to the instructions from the manufacturer. Ultimately, the tissues were counterstained with hematoxylin and mounted for visualization under a light microscope. Image analysis was conducted using ImageJ software.</p></section><section id="sec4dot5-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.5. Serum Alanine Aminotransferase (ALT), Aspartate Aminotransferase (AST), and Alkaline Phosphatase (ALP) Detection</h3>
<p>To evaluate hepatic damage, serum levels of ALT, AST, and ALP were measured. After collecting blood through cardiac puncture, the blood was first deposited at room temperature for 30 min, and then centrifuged at 3000 rpm for 15 min at 4 °C to isolate serum. The enzymatic activities of ALT, AST, and ALP were determined utilizing assay kits obtained from a commercial source (Nanjing Jiancheng Bioengineering Institute, Nanjing, China), adhering strictly to the protocols provided by the manufacturer.</p></section><section id="sec4dot6-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.6. Quantitative Real-Time Polymerase Chain Reaction (qRT-PCR)</h3>
<p>Total RNA was isolated from both hepatic tissue and LX-2 cells employing the TRIzol reagent method (Takara, Japan), and treated with diethylpyrocarbonate (DEPC, BBI Life Sciences, Shanghai, China) to eliminate contaminants. The RNA purity was assessed by determining the absorbance ratio at 260/280 nm with a spectrophotometer (NanoDrop™, Thermo Fisher Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA). Subsequently, residual DNA was degraded using a 5x g-DNA digester Mix (Yeasen Biotechnology Co., Ltd., Shanghai, China) according to the manufacturer’s protocol. 1 µg RNA was reverse transcribed into cDNA using 4x Hifair<sup>®</sup> III SuperMix Plus (Yeasen Biotechnology Co., Ltd., Shanghai, China). Quantitative PCR (qPCR) was conducted using Hieff<sup>®</sup> qPCR SYBR Green Master Mix (Fujian Herui Biotechnology Co., Ltd., Fuzhou, China) on a CFX Connect™ Real-Time System (Bio-Rad Laboratories, Hercules, CA, USA). The identity of the amplified target cDNA was verified through melting curve analysis. Quantification of relative mRNA expression levels was standardized against glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH), employing the 2<sup>−ΔΔCt</sup> calculation approach. Detailed information regarding primer sequences is provided in <a href="#pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t004"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 4.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Primer sequences for real-time reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction.</p></div>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/pharmaceuticals-18-01228-t004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec4dot7-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.7. Quantification of Inflammatory Cytokines by Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay (ELISA)</h3>
<p>The protein levels of inflammatory cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6) in liver tissue homogenates were quantified using commercial ELISA kits (Thermo Fisher Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA) following the manufacturer’s instructions.</p></section><section id="sec4dot8-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.8. Western Blot</h3>
<p>Protein extraction from both CCl<sub>4</sub>-treated murine hepatic tissues and <em>in vitro</em> cultured cells was performed with Radio Immunoprecipitation Assay (RIPA) lysis buffer containing 1% protease and phosphatase inhibitor cocktail. The samples were kept on ice during the extraction process. After centrifugation at 12,000 rpm for 15 min at 4 °C, protein concentrations in the resulting supernatants were determined using a BCA protein quantification kit (HERUI) under the manufacturer’s guidelines. For Western blot analysis, primary antibodies against β-actin (1:2500, abs171598, Absin, Shanghai, China), α-SMA (1:2500, Proteintech Group, Wuhan, China), Collagen I (1:2000, Proteintech Group, Wuhan, China), PDGFR-β (1:1250, Proteintech Group, Wuhan, China), p-PDGFR-β (1:1000, Abcam, UK), GAPDH (1:1500, Servicebio, Wuhan, China), p-AKT (1:1000, Abcam, UK), AKT (1:1000, Abcam, UK), p-ERK (1:1500, Selleck Chemicals, Houston, TX, USA) and ERK (1:1000, Selleck Chemicals, Houston, TX, USA), were applied. Tris-buffered saline (TBS) was prepared by dissolving TBS powder (Biosharp) in double-distilled water. Protein samples (31.25 µg per lane) were subsequently separated by sodium dodecyl sulfate-polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE) and subsequently transferred electrophoretically to polyvinylidene difluoride (PVDF) membranes. Subsequently, 5% non-fat milk or 5% bovine serum albumin (BSA), prepared in Tris-buffered saline with 0.1% Tween-20 (TBS-T), was used to incubate the membranes at room temperature for 1.5 h to achieve blocking. Afterward, the membranes were subjected to overnight incubation with specific primary antibodies at 4 °C (16–24 h), followed by a 1h incubation period at room temperature with corresponding secondary antibodies (Bioss, Beijing, China) at a dilution of 1:3000. After blocking and incubation with the primary or secondary antibodies, TBS-T was applied to wash the PVDF membranes. An enhanced chemiluminescence (ECL) detection system (Life-iLab, Shanghai, China) was employed to visualize the protein bands, and subsequent quantification of band intensity was performed utilizing ImageJ analysis software.</p></section><section id="sec4dot9-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.9. Cell Treatments</h3>
<p>The LX-2 cells were obtained from Procell Life Science &amp; Technology Co., Ltd. (Wuhan, China). The cultivation of LX-2 cells is conducted under controlled conditions at a temperature of 37 °C in an atmosphere enriched with 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. The cells are nurtured in Dulbecco’s Modified Eagle Medium (DMEM, BBI), which is further supplemented with 2% FBS (Nanjing SenBeiJia Biological Technology Co., Ltd., Nanjing, China) and 1% Penicillin-Streptomycin Solution to support their growth and to prevent microbial contamination. Adherent cell growth is monitored daily, and fresh medium is replaced to eliminate necrotic cells. Cells are passaged or digested for subsequent experiments when they reach 80–90% confluence. Cells in the logarithmic growth phase are used for experimentation to ensure the accuracy and reliability of experimental outcomes. To model fibrotic activation, LX-2 cells were stimulated with either (1) 20 ng/mL recombinant human PDGF-BB (TargetMol, MA, USA) for 24 h, or (2) 10% FBS-containing DMEM for 24 h. Control groups received equivalent volumes of serum-free DMEM with vehicle (PBS).</p></section><section id="sec4dot10-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.10. RNA-Sequencing Analysis</h3>
<p>Liver tissues were rapidly excised, homogenized in TRIzol, and snap-frozen in liquid nitrogen. RNA sequencing was performed by Wuhan Kangce Biotechnology Co., Ltd. (Wuhan, China). Sequencing analysis was performed on the Illumina NovaSeq 6000 platform (Illumina, CA, USA). For differential gene expression analysis, the DESeq2 package was implemented. Identification of differentially expressed genes (DEGs) was based on the established criteria of |fold change| ≥ 2 and a false discovery rate (FDR) threshold of &lt; 0.05.</p>
<p>Functional profiling of DEGs was conducted via GSEA and KEGG pathway enrichment to uncover significantly modulated biological processes and pathway signatures. Transcriptomic profile variations between normal control and Eup-treated groups were analyzed through hierarchical clustering and principal component analysis (PCA) approaches. The R package heatmap (R4.4.1) was utilized to generate a graphical representation of DEGs.</p></section><section id="sec4dot11-pharmaceuticals-18-01228"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.11. Statistical Analysis</h3>
<p>GraphPad Prism software (Version 9.0.0, San Diego, CA, USA) was applied for statistical analysis and graphing column charts with individual values. All experimental results are expressed as mean values with standard deviation (mean ± SD). Before statistical comparisons, datasets were subjected to normality assessment using the Shapiro-Wilk test and variance homogeneity verification through Bartlett’s test. Statistical analysis of normally distributed datasets with homogeneous variances across groups (<em>p</em> &gt; 0.05) was performed using one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) with subsequent Tukey’s post hoc testing for multiple comparison analysis. When variance homogeneity assumptions were violated (<em>p</em> &lt; 0.05), non-parametric alternatives, including the Brown-Forsythe test for variance homogeneity and Welch’s ANOVA with Games-Howell post hoc analysis, were employed. Statistical significance was determined at a threshold of <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05. The statistical significances of different grades were defined as * <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05, ** <em>p</em> &lt; 0.01, and *** <em>p</em> &lt; 0.001.</p></section></section><section id="sec23"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusions</h2>
<p>Eup improves CCl<sub>4</sub>-induced liver fibrosis by alleviating hepatic inflammation and suppressing the HSCs activation via modulating the PDGF-BB/PDGFR-β signaling pathway.</p></section><section id="glossary1" class="glossary"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Abbreviations</h2>
